An Achievable Rate Region for 3 − 3- User Classical Quantum Broadcast Channel via Coset Codes
Fatma Gouiaa and Arun Padakandla

Abstract
We undertake a Shannon theoretic study of the problem of communicating statistically independent bit streams over a 3-user classical quantum broadcast channel ( 3 − 3- CQBC) and focus on characterizing inner bounds to its capacity region. We propose a coding strategy based on coset codes possessing algebraic closure properties. Elevating Sen’s technique of tilting, smoothing, and augmentation - originally designed only for IID codes - we design new POVMs that can simultaneously decode into a combination of unstructured IID and coset codes to efficiently decode univariate and bivariate interferences respectively. Analyzing the information-theoretic performance of the proposed coding strategy we characterize a new inner bound to the capacity region of the 3 − 3- CQBC that subsumes all currently known bounds and is proven to be strictly larger for identified examples.



I
Introduction

Even while information theory’s connections
[
1
]
with mathematical physics
[
2
,
3
,
4
]
, information geometry
[
5
,
6
]
, computer science
[
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
]
have blossomed, the problem of characterizing the information carrying capacity of a channel has had a large influence
[
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
,
18
]
on its agenda. In solving this problem via
random coding
in the context of a classical point-to-point channel, Shannon
[
19
]
invented an ingenious and powerful technique. It is indeed remarkable that the average error probability of a code whose codewords are
independent and identically distributed
(IID) with a product distribution falls exponentially for any rate below capacity. Note that codewords of highly probable codes within Shannon’s ensemble possess empirical properties, but are otherwise
bereft of any additional structural properties
. Indeed, we can only state that individual codewords are
typical

[
20
]
with high probability, while unable to make any assertions on their structural relationships or their behaviour when jointly operated via multi-variate functions. Shannon’s recipe of employing unstructured IID codes in characterizing capacity regions has pervaded through all of classical
[
12
,
21
,
13
,
11
,
20
]
and quantum information theory
[
22
,
23
,
17
,
24
,
18
]
and forms the defacto approach in any information theoretic study, be it the one-shot or the asymptotic regime. The problems of characterizing the capacity regions of the general quantum interference and quantum broadcast channels remaining open, it is natural to inquire whether
unstructured IID codes
are optimal for multi-terminal (network) quantum communication?

The broad import of our findings, as we will elaborate in the sequel, establishes that the conventional approach of unstructured IID codes is sub-optimal for network quantum communication. In essence, we prove that multi-terminal quantum channels (q-channels), particularly those with three or more terminals, possess higher degrees of freedom that
cannot
be exploited via unstructured IID codes owing to their lack of additional structural properties. Therefore, to characterize the capacity regions of multi-terminal q-channels and identifying codes that can achieve the same, deliberate attempt must be made to incorporate richer structural properties, in addition to individual empirical ones. Moreover, to harness the full communication capabilities of multi-terminal q-channels, these codes must be coupled with newer encoding maps and decoding POVMs that exploit their structural relationships. Towards stating our specific findings, we begin by describing the q-channel and the problem of interest.

Consider a
3-
user classical-quantum broadcast channel (
3-
CQBC) with three distributed receivers (Rxs) depicted in Fig.
1
. The transmitter (Tx) needs to communicate three statistically independent bit streams in an information-theoretically reliably sense
[
25
]
to the three Rxs. The problem of interest is to characterize the capacity region of the
3-
CQBC. Our specific focus is on designing coding strategies, analyzing their performance and deriving
inner bounds
to the capacity region of the
3-
CQBC.

Fig. 1:
Three independent messages to be communicated over a
3-
CQBC.

What is the current known best coding strategy for communicating over a
3-
CQBC? Marton’s strategy
[
12
]
- a combination of Cover’s superposition coding
[
21
]
and precoding
[
12
]
via binning - designed in the context of a
2-
user classical broadcast channel (BC)
[
20
, Ch. 8]
is the current known best
[
17
,
26
]
for any quantum BC with any number of Rxs. Recognizing this, Savov and Wilde
[
17
]
design appropriate decoding POVMs to adopt Marton’s strategy for communicating over a general
2
Rx CQBC (
2-
CQBC). Identifying necessary analytical tools such as the
overcounting
to analyze its performance, they
[
17
]
prove achievability of an inner bound which is a natural CQ generalization of Marton’s inner bound
[
12
]
. The latter is indeed the current known largest inner bound to the capacity region of a general
2-
CQBC. A natural generalization of their inner bound
[
17
]
to the three Rx case yields the current known largest for a
3-
CQBC, which we henceforth refer to as the unstructured inner bound. A characterization of this latter inner bound can be obtained by replacing the information quantities in
[
27
, Thm. 1]
with appropriate Holevo informations.

Taking a cue from
[
27
]
and building on our prior works
[
28
,
29
]

we step beyond unstructured IID code strategies to design a coding strategy based on jointly designed coset codes. As we discuss in the sequel, communication over
3-
CQBC requires design of multiple codes. In an unstructured IID code strategy, the multiple codes possess no inter-relationships. Moreover, codewords of any code possess no intra-relationships. Here, we design a coding strategy wherein codes are chosen to be
cosets
of a
linear
code and therefore algebraically closed. Moreover,
specific
pairs of codes are picked as cosets of a
common linear code
endowing them with inter-relationships (Rem.
4
). See Fig.
9
. We exploit these joint structural relationships by designing new
simultaneous
decoding POVMs that can efficiently recover bivariate functions. Identifying appropriate analytical tools to characterize information theoretic performance of the designed coding strategy, we derive a new inner bound (Thms.
3
,
4
) to the capacity region of the
3-
CQBC that subsumes the current known largest - the unstructured inner bound (Rem.
6
). Moreover, we identify Ex.
1
for which the derived inner bound is proven (Lem.
1
) to be strictly larger than the latter.

We encounter three challenges in design and analysis of the above coding strategy for a general
3-
CQBC. Firstly, since each Rx must decode into several codebooks, we are required to design and analyze POVMs that can
simultaneously decode
. In particular, the advantages of jointly designed coset codes crucially relies on the Rx decoding the bivariate function, as against to the pair of its input arguments. This will require decoding into ‘functions of codebooks’ - the codeword collection obtained by applying the bivariate function on each pair of codewords. Moreover, a general coding strategy must involve two layers - one comprising of unstructured IID code to enable Rxs decode univariate interference components and the other of jointly designed coset codes. Rxs must therefore decode
simultaneously
into a
combination of both coset and unstructured IID codes
. Design and analysis of simultaneously decoding POVMs even into the well studied unstructured IID codes has proven to be a difficult challenge. Indeed, this challenge remained open for over a decade since Winter’s recognition
[
22
, Sec. VIII]
of the same, spurring several alternate approaches
[
23
,
30
]
. Recently, Sen has invented
tilting, smoothing, and augmentation
(TSA)
[
24
,
18
]
to design and analyze a quantum simultaneous decoder. Sen’s technique
[
24
,
18
]
is remarkably powerful and accomplishes simultaneous decoding even in the more general one-shot regime. However, Sen’s study is confined to decoding into unstructured IID codes. Our first challenge is to design and analyze a quantum decoder that can
simultaneously decode
into ‘functions of codebooks’, jointly designed coset
and
unstructured IID codes to recover bivariate functions efficiently.

The second challenge pertains to analyzing binning in a
3-
user quantum BC. Since binning entails exponentially many codewords for each message, its analysis over a q-channel results in a positive exponent appearing in the error probability. This has resulted in mis-steps
[
17
, Sec. I and Note therein]
. Savov and Wilde
[
17
]
propose an
overcounting
technique to suppress this positive exponent. While this overcounting technique works for the
2-
CQBC, it leaves the challenge unresolved in the
3-
user case, as elaborated in
[
31
, Sec. 3]
. We also note here that the analysis steps presented in
[
31
]
needs a clearer mathematical justification. See
[
32
]
. Since binning is an integral part of any general coding strategy involving coset codes, we are required to identify a robust technique to analyze the same that is compatible with the new quantum simultaneous decoder that we design. It is important to note that the above two challenges are specific to the non-commutative nature of the
3-
CQBC and hence not encountered in
[
27
,
28
]
. Thirdly, note that codewords of a random coset code are uniformly distributed and only pairwise independent. Achieving inner bounds corresponding to arbitrary distributions over generic
3-
CQBC cannot proceed as in the case of unstructured IID codes, since the latter can be chosen with respect to any product distribution.

We overcome the first challenge by elevating Sen’s TSA
[
24
,
18
]
to incorporate efficient bivariate function decoding. Specifically, we first resort back to appropriate conditional typical projectors that can decode bivariate functions. Next, as elaborated in proof of Thm.
2
, we identify new auxiliary spaces and new tilting maps to decode the bivariate function and the unstructured codeword simultaneously. To address the second challenge, we put forth an alternate approach to analyze binning over any q-channel. Recognizing the connection of binning to soft covering
[
33
, Lemma 19]
, we employ a stochastic encoder that enables us decouple the binning and channel coding analysis in a way that works for any scenario involving binning over classical or q-channel. Our ability to incorporate and analyze all these different elements - coset codes, simultaneous decoding, binning - in a single coding scenario in a rigorous information-theoretic way constitutes an important element of our work.

Our findings build on considerable prior work. The problem of characterizing bit carrying capacity of network (multi-terminal) q-channels gathered momentum following Holevo
[
15
]
, Schumacher and Westmoreland’s
[
16
]
characterization of the CQ capacity of a point-to-point (PTP) q-channel. In their study of a more general CQ PTP q-channel, Hayashi and Nagaoka identified powerful analytical tools
[
34
]
that form a central part of CQ capacity studies. Winter’s study of the CQ capacity of a quantum MAC
[
22
, Sec. VIII]
revealed challenges of analyzing a quantum simultaneous decoder. The study of quantum broadcast channel (QBC) was first initiated by Dupuis, Hayden and Li
[
35
]
with the additional resource of entanglement. This was furthered by Yard, Hayden and Devetak
[
26
]
who studied the use of QBC to communicate both bits and qubits. Savov and Wilde
[
17
]
proved achievability of the CQ analogue of Marton’s inner bound for the
2-
CQBC. Recently, Salek, Hayden and Hayashi undertake information theoretic study
[
31
]
of multi-level QBCs. Sen proved achievability of Han-Kobayashi’s inner bound
[
13
]
in the asymptotic regime via sequential decoding. Inventing the techniques of TSA
[
24
]
, Sen proved achievability of inner bounds
[
18
]
for several two-terminal CQ channels in the more general one-shot regime. We should note that all of the above works employ unstructured IID codes. In regards to binning and our approach to overcome the challenge over q-channels, we note that
[
36
]
,
[
37
]
and
[
38
]
have developed analogous techniques in the context of source coding problems.

We highlight the significance of our findings. Characterizing the capacity region of a BC is regarded as a fundamental problem
[
20
]
both in classical
[
39
]
and quantum information theory. Indeed, this problem has continued to receive attention and its pursuit has yielded new coding strategies that are applicable in other channel scenarios. Secondly, the use of unstructured IID codes is ubiquitous in information theoretic studies. The restriction of earlier studies
[
17
,
23
]
to two Rx channels belie an underlying assumption that optimal coding strategies for two Rx channels can be naturally extended to yield optimal strategies for channels with three or more Rxs and that the latter do not possess any newer aspects that require more sophisticated coding strategies. By combining challenging elements such as simultaneous decoding, coset codes and new decoding POVMs, characterizing new achievable rate regions that subsume the current known largest and strictly enlarging the same for examples, our findings put to rest such an assumption and establish that multi-terminal channels admit newer coding strategies.

Organization
: Following preliminaries - notation and problem statement - in Sec.
II-A
, we propose a technique based on likelihood encoder and analysis via soft covering to analyze binning in the context of a
2-
CQBC in Sec.
II-B
that we shall employ in proofs of subsequent theorems. In Sec.
III-A
, considering a specific example, we explain how coset codes possessing algebraic closure properties can outperform unstructured IID codes for communicating over
3-
CQBCs. Following this discussion, in Sec.
III-B
we outline the structure of a general coding strategy for communicating over a general
3-
CQBC. We design and analyze this coding strategy in three pedagogical steps. Step I, presented in Sec.
IV
, enables one of the Rxs to decode bivariate interference. In Step II presented in Sec.
V-B
, a full suite of coset codes is employed to enable each Rx decode bivariate interference. We combine the strategy of Step II with unstructured IID codes in Step III to design and analyze a general coding strategy that enables each Rx decode univariate and bivariate interferences efficiently. The characterized inner bound therein (Thm.
3
) subsumes all current known inner bounds and is strictly larger for Ex.
1
. We remark that this combination is analogous to the early work of Ahlswede and Han
[
40
]
in the context of distributed source coding.

II
Preliminaries and Problem Statement

II-A

Notation and Problem Statement

We supplement notation in
[
25
]
with the following. For
K\in\mathbb{N}
,
[K]\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left\{1,\ldots,K\right\}
. For prime
\upsilon\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}
will denote the finite field of size
\upsilon
with
\oplus
denoting field addition in
\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}
(i.e., mod
-\upsilon
). For a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}),\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
denote the collection of linear, positive and density operators acting on
\mathcal{H}
respectively.
We let an
underline
denote an appropriate aggregation of objects. For example,
\underline{\mathcal{V}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mathcal{V}_{1}\times\mathcal{V}_{2}\times\mathcal{V}_{3}
,
\underline{v}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})\in\underline{\mathcal{V}}
and in regards to Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{Y_{i}}:i\in[3]
, we let
\mathcal{H}_{\underline{Y}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\otimes_{i=1}^{3}\mathcal{H}_{Y_{i}}
. Let
*
denote the binary convolution:
p*q\coloneqq p(1-q)+(1-p)q
. For
p\in[0,1]
,
h_{b}(p)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}-p\log_{2}p-(1-p)\log_{2}(1-p)
denote the binary entropy function. For
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
,
\|A\|_{1}
denotes the trace norm and
\|A\|_{\infty}
denotes the operator norm. A positive operator valued measurement (POVM) is a collection of positive operators that sum to the identity operator. When we say a POVM
\theta_{\mathcal{A}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\theta_{a}:a\in\mathcal{A}\}
, we assume that
\theta_{a}
is positive for
a\in\mathcal{A}
and
\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\theta_{a}=I
where
\mathcal{A}
is a finite set. We abbreviate probability mass function, conditional typical projector, and unconditional typical projector as PMF, C-Typ-Proj and U-Typ-Proj respectively.

Note 1
.

Our work lies in quantum information theory (QIT) and we have strived to employ notation that is consistent with related articles such as
[
41
,
23
,
17
,
25
]
. As observed in these articles
[
41
,
23
,
17
,
25
]
, the convention is to denote matrices such as density operators and POVMs via regular faced alphabets as against to bold-faced ones. In the interest of consistency, we have thus not adopted bold-faced characters for matrices as recommended
here
.

Consider a (generic)
3-
CQBC

(\rho_{x}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\underline{Y}}):x\in\mathcal{X},\kappa)
specified through (i) a finite set
\mathcal{X}
, (ii) Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{Y_{j}}:j\in[3]
, (iii) a collection
(\rho_{x}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\underline{Y}}):x\in\mathcal{X})
and (iv) a cost function
\kappa:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
. The cost function is assumed to be additive, i.e., the cost of preparing the state
\otimes_{t=1}^{n}\rho_{x_{t}}
is
\overline{\kappa}^{n}(x^{n})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\kappa(x_{t})
. Reliable communication on a
3-
CQBC entails identifying a code.

Definition 1
.

A
3-
CQBC code
c=(n,\underline{\mathcal{M}},e,\uline{\mu})
consists of (i) three message index sets
\mathcal{M}_{j}:j\in[3]
, (ii) an encoder map
e:\mathcal{M}_{1}\times\mathcal{M}_{2}\times\mathcal{M}_{3}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}^{n}
and (iii) POVMs
\mu_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\mu_{j,m_{j}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{Y_{j}}^{\otimes n}):m_{j}\in\mathcal{M}_{j}\}:j\in[3]
. The average probability of error of the
3-
CQBC code
(n,\underline{\mathcal{M}},e,\uline{\mu})
is

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(e,\uline{\mu})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}1-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}_{1}||\mathcal{M}_{2}||\mathcal{M}_{3}|}\sum_{\underline{m}\in\underline{\mathcal{M}}}\tr\left\{\left(\mu_{1,m_{1}}\otimes\mu_{2,m_{2}}\otimes\mu_{3,m_{3}}\right)\left(\otimes_{t=1}^{n}\rho_{x_{t}(\underline{m})}\right)\right\},

where
\uline{\mu}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\otimes_{j=1}^{3}\mu_{j}
and
x^{n}(\underline{m})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}(x_{t}(\underline{m}):1\leq t\leq n)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}e(\underline{m})
. The average cost per symbol of transmitting message
\underline{m}\in\underline{\mathcal{M}}
is

\tau(e|\underline{m})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\overline{\kappa}^{n}(e(\underline{m}))
, and the average cost per symbol of
3-
CQBC code is
\tau(e)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{1}{|\underline{\mathcal{M}}|}\sum_{\underline{m}\in\underline{\mathcal{M}}}\tau(e|\underline{m})
.

Definition 2
.

A rate-cost quadruple
(R_{1},R_{2},R_{3},\tau)\in[0,\infty)^{4}
is achievable if there exists a sequence of
3-
CQBC codes
(n,\underline{\mathcal{M}}^{(n)},e^{(n)},\uline{\mu}^{(n)})
for which
\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{P}(e^{(n)},\uline{\mu}^{(n)})=0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}n^{-1}\log|\mathcal{M}_{j}^{(n)}|=R_{j}:j\in[3],\mbox{ and }\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\tau(e^{(n)})\leq\tau.

The capacity region
\mathscr{C}
of a
3-
CQBC is the set of all achievable rate-cost vectors and
\mathscr{C}(\tau)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\underline{R}:(\underline{R},\tau)\in\mathscr{C}\}
.

Our focus in this article is the capacity region
\mathscr{C}(\tau)
of a generic
3-
CQBC. Specifically, our goal is to provide a single-letter characterization of an inner bound to
\mathscr{C}(\tau)
. The main novelty of our work is the design of a coding scheme based on nested coset codes (NCCs) which we define below.

Definition 3
.

An
(n,k,g,b^{n})
coset code built over a finite field
\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}
is specified via (i) a generator matrix
g\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{k\times n}
and (ii) a bias
b^{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{n}
. We let
u^{n}(a)=ag\oplus b^{n}
for
a\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{k}
denote the codewords of the coset code
(n,k,g,b^{n})
, where as mentioned above
\oplus
denotes field addition in
\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}
. The rate of this code is
\frac{k}{n}\log(\Prime)
.

Definition 4
.

An
(n,k,l,g_{I},g_{O/I},b^{n})
NCC over
\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}
consists of (i) generator matrices
g_{I}\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{k\times n}
and
g_{O/I}\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{l\times n}
, and
(ii) bias vector
b^{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{n}
. We let
u^{n}(a,m)=ag_{I}\oplus mg_{O/I}\oplus b^{n}
, for
(a,m)\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{k}\times\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{l}
denote codewords of the coset code. The collection
\left(u^{n}(a,m)\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{n}:a\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{k}\right)
is referred to as the coset corresponding to message
m\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{l}
.

II-B

Analysis of Marton’s binning of channel code via a likelihood encoder

The technique of Marton’s binning will play a central role throughout this article. As discussed in Sec.
I
we need a robust/general approach to analyze binning in diverse quantum channel coding scenarios. In this section we describe a likelihood encoder coupled with corresponding analysis steps that can enable one to analyze binning in
any
channel coding scenario, classical or quantum. We describe the same in the context of analyzing Marton’s binning for the
2-
CQBC. As will be evident to an informed reader, the following steps decouple the encoding and decoding analysis in a way (see Rem.
2
) so as to permit its applicability in any channel coding scenario. We begin with the corresponding necessary definitions of
2-
CQBC.

Consider a (generic)
2\mathchar 45\relax
CQBC

(\rho_{x}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{Y_{2}}):x\in\mathcal{X},\kappa)
specified through (i) a finite set
\mathcal{X}
, (ii) Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{Y_{j}}:j\in[2]
, (iii) a collection
(\rho_{x}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{Y_{2}}):x\in\mathcal{X})
and (iv) a cost function
\kappa:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
. The cost function is assumed to be additive, i.e., the cost of preparing the state
\otimes_{t=1}^{n}\rho_{x_{t}}
is
\overline{\kappa}^{n}(x^{n})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\kappa(x_{t})
. Reliable communication on a
2\mathchar 45\relax
CQBC entails identifying a code.

Definition 5
.

A
2\mathchar 45\relax
CQBC code
c=(n,\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2},e,\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{2})
consists of (i) two message index sets
\mathcal{M}_{j}:j\in[2]
, (ii) an encoder map
e:\mathcal{M}_{1}\times\mathcal{M}_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}^{n}
and (iii) POVMs
\mu_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\mu_{j,m_{j}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{Y_{j}}^{\otimes n}):m_{j}\in\mathcal{M}_{j}\}:j\in[2]
. The average probability of error of the
2\mathchar 45\relax
CQBC code
(n,\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2},e,\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{2})
is

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(e,\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{2})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}1-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}_{1}||\mathcal{M}_{2}|}\sum_{m_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}}\sum_{m_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{2}}\tr\left\{\left(\mu_{1,m_{1}}\otimes\mu_{2,m_{2}}\right)\left(\otimes_{t=1}^{n}\rho_{x_{t}(m_{1},m_{2})}\right)\right\},

where
x^{n}(m_{1},m_{2})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}(x_{t}(m_{1},m_{2}):1\leq t\leq n)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}e(m_{1},m_{2})
. Average cost per symbol of transmitting message
(m_{1},m_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{1}\times\mathcal{M}_{2}
is
\tau(e|m_{1},m_{2})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\overline{\kappa}^{n}(e(m_{1},m_{2}))
and the average cost per symbol of
2\mathchar 45\relax
CQBC code is
\tau(e)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}_{1}||\mathcal{M}_{2}|}\sum_{m_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}}\sum_{m_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{2}}\tau(e|m_{1},m_{2})
.

Definition 6
.

A rate-cost triple
(R_{1},R_{2},\tau)\in[0,\infty)^{3}
is achievable if there exists a sequence of
2\mathchar 45\relax
CQBC codes
(n,\mathcal{M}_{1}^{(n)},\mathcal{M}_{2}^{(n)},e^{(n)},\left(\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{2}\right)^{(n)})
for which
\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{P}(e^{(n)},\left(\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{2}\right)^{(n)})=0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}n^{-1}\log|\mathcal{M}_{j}^{(n)}|=R_{j}:j\in[2],\mbox{ and }\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\tau(e^{(n)})\leq\tau.

The capacity region
\mathscr{C}
of a
2-
CQBC is the set of all achievable rate-cost vectors and
\mathscr{C}(\tau)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{(R_{1},R_{2}):(R_{1},R_{2},\tau)\in\mathscr{C}\}
.

Theorem 1
.

A rate-cost triple
(R_{1},R_{2},\tau)\in[0,\infty)^{3}
is achievable if there exists (i) two finite sets
\mathcal{V}_{1}
,
\mathcal{V}_{2}
, (ii) a PMF
p_{V_{1}V_{2}}
on
\mathcal{V}_{1}\times\mathcal{V}_{2}
and (iii) a mapping function
f:\mathcal{V}_{1}\times\mathcal{V}_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
such that
\sum_{(v_{1},v_{2})}p_{V_{1}V_{2}}(v_{1},v_{2})\kappa(f(v_{1},v_{2}))\leq\tau
,

\displaystyle R_{1}+R_{2}<\sum_{j=1}^{2}I(V_{j};Y_{j})-I(V_{1};V_{2}),\mbox{ and }R_{j}<I(V_{j};Y_{j}),\mbox{ for }j=1,2

holds, where all the information quantities are computed with respect to the state

\displaystyle\rho^{V_{1}V_{2}XY_{1}Y_{2}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sum_{(v_{1},v_{2},x)}p_{V_{1}V_{2}X}(v_{1},v_{2},x)\outerproduct{v_1,v_2,x}{v_1,v_2,x}\otimes\rho_{x}^{Y_{1}Y_{2}},

(1)

with
p_{V_{1}V_{2}X}(v_{1},v_{2},x)=p_{V_{1}V_{2}}(v_{1},v_{2})\mathds{1}\{x=f(v_{1},v_{2})\},
for
(v_{1},v_{2},x)\in\mathcal{V}_{1}\times\mathcal{V}_{2}\times\mathcal{X}
.

Proof.

Consider a pair
\mathcal{V}_{j}:j=1,2
of finite sets, a generic function
f:\mathcal{V}_{1}\times\mathcal{V}_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
and a generic PMF
p_{V_{1}V_{2}}
on
\mathcal{V}_{1}\times\mathcal{V}_{2}
that satisfy the constraints stated in the hypothesis, i.e. the average cost
\sum_{(v_{1},v_{2})}p_{V_{1}V_{2}}(v_{1},v_{2})\kappa(f(v_{1},v_{2}))\leq\tau
. Let
(R_{1},R_{2})
be a rate pair that satisfy the corresponding bounds in Thm.
1
. We divide the proof into four parts entailing the code structure, encoding rule, decoding POVMs and error analysis.

Code structure:

For
j=1,2
, the code employed to communicate Rx
j
’s message comprises (i) a collection
c_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{v_{j}^{n}(b_{j},m_{j}):b_{j}\in[2^{nK_{j}}],m_{j}\in[2^{nR_{j}}]\}
constructed over the alphabet
\mathcal{V}_{j}^{n}
and (ii) a binning map
b_{j}:[2^{nR_{1}}]\times[2^{nR_{2}}]\rightarrow[2^{nK_{j}}]
. For
j=1,2,
let
c_{j}(m_{j})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}(v_{j}^{n}(b_{j},m_{j}):b_{j}\in[2^{nK_{j}}])
denote the bin corresponding to message
m_{j}
. Conventionally, the codewords in
c_{j}
are picked IID with distribution
\prod_{t=1}^{n}p_{X_{j}}
. However, to demonstrate the generality of our approach, we pick these codewords, as will be formalized when we analyze the error probability, IID with an alternate distribution
\prod_{t=1}^{n}q_{V_{j}}
.
Secondly, the binning map, which identifies the chosen codewords, is conventionally based on typicality. In other words,
(b_{1}(m_{1},m_{2}),b_{2}(m_{1},m_{2}))\in[2^{nK_{1}}]\times[2^{nK_{2}}]
is chosen deterministically to ensure
v_{j}^{n}(b_{j}(m_{1},m_{2}),m_{j}):j=1,2
is jointly typical with respect to
p_{V_{1}V_{2}}
. A corresponding randomization - uniformly chosen amongst all joint typical pairs - is employed to analyze error probability. Instead of forcing typicality, as is done conventionally, we employ a soft probabilistic approach inspired by Cuff’s likelihood encoder
[
42
]
for choosing the pair of codewords. This will be evident when we specify the distribution with respect to which the binning map
b_{j}:[2^{nR_{1}}]\times[2^{nR_{2}}]\rightarrow[2^{nK_{j}}]
is chosen.

Encoding Rule:

For each message pair
(m_{1},m_{2})
, the encoder chooses codeword pair
\left(v_{j}^{n}(b_{j}^{n}(m_{1},m_{2}),m_{j}):j=1,2\right)
.
To communicate the messages
(m_{1},m_{2})
, the encoder prepares the quantum state
\rho_{x^{n}(m_{1},m_{2})}^{Y_{1}Y_{2}}
where

\displaystyle x^{n}(m_{1},m_{2})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}f^{n}\left(v_{1}^{n}(b_{1}(m_{1},m_{2}),m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(b_{2}(m_{1},m_{2}),m_{2})\right).

with
f
evaluated letter-by-letter.

Decoding POVMs:

The decoding POVMs for Rx
1
and Rx
2
being identical, we present the same in terms of a generic index
j
. Let
\pi_{v_{j}^{n}}
and
\pi
denote the C-TYP-Proj and U-TYP-Proj with respect to the state
\rho_{v_{j}^{n}}
and
\rho
respectively, where
\rho_{v_{j}^{n}}=\bigotimes_{t=1}^{n}\rho_{v_{j_{t}}}
and for all
v_{j}\in\mathcal{V}_{j}
,
\rho_{v_{j}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sum_{v_{\text{\sout{$j$}}}}p_{V_{\text{\sout{$j$}}}|V_{j}}(v_{\text{\sout{$j$}}}|v_{j})\rho_{f(v_{1},v_{2})}^{Y_{j}}
, where
\text{\sout{$j$}}\in\{1,2\}\backslash\{j\}
denotes the complement index, i.e.,
\{j,\text{\sout{$j$}}\}=\{1,2\}
, and
\rho\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sum_{v_{1},v_{2}}p_{V_{1}V_{2}}(v_{1},v_{2})\rho_{f(v_{1},v_{2})}^{Y_{j}}
.
We define the square-root measurement
[
25
,
43
]

\{\mu_{m_{j}}:m_{j}\in[2^{nR_{j}}]\}
to decode
m_{j}
as

\displaystyle\hskip-21.68121pt\mu_{m_{j}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left(\sum_{(\tilde{b}_{j},\tilde{m}_{j})}\gamma_{\tilde{b}_{j},\tilde{m}_{j}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{b_{j}}\gamma_{b_{j},m_{j}}\right)\left(\sum_{(\tilde{b}_{j},\tilde{m}_{j})}\gamma_{\tilde{b}_{j},\tilde{m}_{j}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\!\!,\mbox{ and }\mu_{-1}^{Y_{j}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}I^{Y_{j}}-\sum_{m_{j}}\mu_{m_{j}},\mbox{ where }\gamma_{b_{j},m_{j}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\pi\pi_{v_{j}^{n}(b_{j},m_{j})}\pi.

Error analysis:

As is standard in information theory, we derive an upper bound on the error probability of a good code by averaging the error probability over an ensemble of codes. We begin by specifying the codebook distribution, i.e, the random code distribution, with respect to which this averaging is performed. Recall that our codes and the coding scheme are completely specified via the objects:
c_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{v_{j}^{n}(b_{j},m_{j}):b_{j}\in[2^{nK_{j}}],m_{j}\in[2^{nR_{j}}]\},
and
b_{j}:[2^{nR_{1}}]\times[2^{nR_{2}}]\rightarrow[2^{nK_{j}}]
for
j=1,2
. It therefore suffices to specify a joint distribution of these objects. For any choice of the arguments in their respective range spaces, let

\displaystyle\hskip 36.135ptP\left(\begin{aligned} &\left(V_{j}^{n}(b_{j},m_{j})=v_{j}^{n}(b_{j},m_{j}):b_{j}\in[2^{nK_{j}}],m_{j}\in[2^{nR_{j}}]\right),\\
&\left(B_{j}(m_{1},m_{2})=b_{j}(m_{1},m_{2}):(m_{1},m_{2})\in[2^{nR_{1}}]\times[2^{nR_{2}}]\right):j=1,2\end{aligned}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip-21.68121pt=\left[\prod_{m_{1}=1}^{2^{nR_{1}}}\prod_{b_{1}=1}^{2^{nK_{1}}}q_{V_{1}}^{n}(v_{1}^{n}(b_{1},m_{1}))\right]\left[\prod_{m_{2}=1}^{2^{nR_{2}}}\prod_{b_{2}=1}^{2^{nK_{2}}}q_{V_{2}}^{n}(v_{2}^{n}(b_{2},m_{2}))\right]\left[\prod_{m_{1}=1}^{2^{nR_{1}}}\prod_{m_{2}=1}^{2^{nR_{2}}}\frac{r_{V_{1}V_{2}}^{n}(v_{1}^{n}(b_{1}(m_{1},m_{2}),m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(b_{2}(m_{1},m_{2}),m_{2}))}{\sum_{(l_{1},l_{2})}r_{V_{1}V_{2}}^{n}(v_{1}^{n}(l_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(l_{2},m_{2}))}\right],

\displaystyle\hskip-21.68121pt\mbox{ where }r_{V_{1}V_{2}}^{n}(v_{1}^{n},v_{2}^{n})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{p_{V_{1}V_{2}}^{n}(v_{1}^{n},v_{2}^{n})}{q_{V_{1}}^{n}(v_{1}^{n})q_{V_{2}}^{n}(v_{2}^{n})},\mbox{ for }(v_{1}^{n},v_{2}^{n})\in\mathcal{V}_{1}\times\mathcal{V}_{2},

(2)

specify our joint codebook distribution.
From the above distribution of the code, it is evident that each of the codewords in the two codes has been picked independently IID according to the respective distributions. Furthermore, the distribution of the chosen codewords conditional on the message and the coset is highlighted in the following remark.

Remark 1
.

As specified when we described the code structure, the distribution of our binning map conditioned on the code distinguishes our encoder from conventional joint typicality-based encoders. Specifically, note from (
2
) that

\displaystyle P\left(B_{1}(m_{1},m_{2})=b_{1},B_{2}(m_{1},m_{2})=b_{2}|C_{j}(m_{j})=(v_{j}^{n}(b_{j},m_{j}):b_{j}\in[2^{nK_{j}}]):j=1,2\right)

(3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{r_{V_{1}V_{2}}^{n}(v_{1}^{n}(b_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(b_{2},m_{2}))}{\sum_{(l_{1},l_{2})}r_{V_{1}V_{2}}^{n}(v_{1}^{n}(l_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(l_{2},m_{2}))}.

We highlight that the pair of
V_{1},V_{2}-
codewords is chosen via a soft likelihood encoder whose distribution is proportional to
r_{V_{1}V_{2}}^{n}(.,.)
. The numerator and denominator of
r_{V_{1}V_{2}}(.)
exemplify the acceptance of
p_{V_{1}V_{2}}
and rejection of
q_{V_{1}}q_{V_{2}}
. This choice of
r_{V_{1}V_{2}}(.)
is inspired from Cuff’s likelihood encoder
[
42
]
.

We now derive an upper bound on the average error probability of a good code.

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(e,\uline{\mu})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{n(R_{1}+R_{2})}}\sum_{m_{1}}\sum_{m_{2}}\tr\left\{\left(I^{Y_{1}Y_{2}}-\mu_{m_{1}}\otimes\mu_{m_{2}}\right)\rho_{e(m_{1},m_{2})}^{Y_{1}Y_{2}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle T_{1}+T_{2},\mbox{ where }

\displaystyle T_{j}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{n(R_{1}+R_{2})}}\sum_{m_{1},m_{2}}\tr\left\{\left(I^{Y_{j}}-\Gamma_{m_{j}}\right)\rho_{e(m_{1},m_{2})}^{Y_{j}}\right\},\mbox{and }

\displaystyle\Gamma_{m_{j}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{(\tilde{b}_{j},\tilde{m}_{j})}\gamma_{\tilde{b}_{j},\tilde{m}_{j}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\gamma_{b_{j}(m_{1},m_{2}),m_{j}}\left(\sum_{(\tilde{b}_{j},\tilde{m}_{j})}\gamma_{\tilde{b}_{j},\tilde{m}_{j}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}},\mbox{ for }j=1,2.

This can be verified via the standard quantum union bound (
21
) and the fact that
\mu_{m_{j}}\geq\Gamma_{m_{j}},
for
j=1,2
.

For
j=1,2
, observe that,

\displaystyle T_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{n(R_{1}+R_{2})}}\sum_{m_{1}}\sum_{m_{2}}\sum_{b_{1}}\sum_{b_{2}}\mathds{1}\{b_{1}(m_{1},m_{2})=b_{1},b_{2}(m_{1},m_{2})=b_{2}\}\tr\left\{\left(I^{Y_{j}}-\Gamma_{m_{j}}\right)\rho_{f^{n}\left(v_{1}^{n}(b_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(b_{2},m_{2})\right)}^{Y_{j}}\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{(*)}{=}

\displaystyle T_{j.1}+T_{j.2},\mbox{ where}

\displaystyle T_{j.1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{n(R_{1}+R_{2})}}\sum_{m_{1}}\sum_{m_{2}}\sum_{b_{1}}\sum_{b_{2}}\left[\mathds{1}\{b_{1}(m_{1},m_{2})=b_{1},b_{2}(m_{1},m_{2})=b_{2}\}-\frac{r_{V_{1}V_{2}}^{n}(v_{1}^{n}(b_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(b_{2},m_{2}))}{2^{n(K_{1}+K_{2})}}\right]

\displaystyle\tr\left\{\left(I^{Y_{j}}-\Gamma_{m_{j}}\right)\rho_{f^{n}\left(v_{1}^{n}(b_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(b_{2},m_{2})\right)}^{Y_{j}}\right\},\mbox{ and }

\displaystyle T_{j.2}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{n(R_{1}+R_{2})}}\sum_{m_{1}}\sum_{m_{2}}\sum_{b_{1}}\sum_{b_{2}}\frac{r_{V_{1}V_{2}}^{n}(v_{1}^{n}(b_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(b_{2},m_{2}))}{2^{n(K_{1}+K_{2})}}\tr\left\{\left(I^{Y_{j}}-\Gamma_{m_{j}}\right)\rho_{f^{n}\left(v_{1}^{n}(b_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(b_{2},m_{2})\right)}^{Y_{j}}\right\}.

The equality
(*)
follows by adding and subtracting

\displaystyle\frac{r_{V_{1}V_{2}}^{n}\left(v_{1}^{n}(b_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(b_{2},m_{2})\right)}{2^{n(K_{1}+K_{2})}},

(4)

thereby decomposing
T_{j}
into two terms
T_{j.1}
and
T_{j.2}
described above.

Remark 2
.

We highlight the above technique of adding and subtracting the term in (
4
) which enables us to decompose the error into two terms,
T_{j.1}
is analyzed using the soft-covering lemma and
T_{j.2}
using a conventional channel coding approach. This enables us to break the statistical dependence. Moreover, bounding
\frac{q_{V_{j}}(.)}{p_{V_{j}}(.)}
appropriately via the divergence (see equation (
51
)) enable us to obtain the right channel coding bound. This approach essentially decouples the binning analysis and the channel coding analysis, thus leaving the channel coding analysis oblivious to the presence of binning.

The term
T_{j.1}
is simply bounded by a covering argument
[
33
]
referred to theirin as ‘cloud mixing’. Observe that,

\displaystyle T_{j.1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{n(R_{1}+R_{2})}}\sum_{m_{1}}\sum_{m_{2}}\sum_{b_{1}}\sum_{b_{2}}\left|\mathds{1}\{b_{1}(m_{1},m_{2})=b_{1},b_{2}(m_{1},m_{2})=b_{2}\}-\frac{r_{V_{1}V_{2}}^{n}(v_{1}^{n}(b_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(b_{2},m_{2}))}{2^{n(K_{1}+K_{2})}}\right|

\displaystyle\left|\tr\left\{\left(I^{Y_{j}}-\Gamma_{m_{j}}\right)\rho_{f^{n}\left(v_{1}^{n}(b_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(b_{2},m_{2})\right)}^{Y_{j}}\right\}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{n(R_{1}+R_{2})}}\sum_{m_{1}}\sum_{m_{2}}\sum_{b_{1}}\sum_{b_{2}}\left|\mathds{1}\{b_{1}(m_{1},m_{2})=b_{1},b_{2}(m_{1},m_{2})=b_{2}\}-\frac{r_{V_{1}V_{2}}^{n}(v_{1}^{n}(b_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(b_{2},m_{2}))}{2^{n(K_{1}+K_{2})}}\right|.

We evaluate the expectation over
C_{j}(m_{j})=\left(V_{j}^{n}(b_{j},m_{j}):b_{j}\in[2^{nK_{j}}]\right):j=1,2
. We obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[T_{1.1}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{n(R_{1}+R_{2})}}\sum_{m_{1}}\sum_{m_{2}}\sum_{b_{1}}\sum_{b_{2}}\sum_{c_{1}(m_{1})}\sum_{c_{2}(m_{2})}P\left(C_{j}(m_{j})=\left(v_{j}^{n}(b_{j},m_{j}):b_{j}\in[2^{nK_{j}}]\right):j=1,2\right)

\displaystyle\hskip-57.81621pt\left|P\left(B_{1}(m_{1},m_{2})=b_{1},B_{2}(m_{1},m_{2})=b_{2}\big|C_{j}(m_{j})=(v_{j}^{n}(b_{j},m_{j}):b_{j}\in[2^{nK_{j}}]):j=1,2\right)-\frac{r_{V_{1}V_{2}}^{n}(v_{1}^{n}(b_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(b_{2},m_{2}))}{2^{n(K_{1}+K_{2})}}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{n(R_{1}+R_{2})}}\sum_{m_{1}}\sum_{m_{2}}\sum_{b_{1}}\sum_{b_{2}}\sum_{c_{1}(m_{1})}\sum_{c_{2}(m_{2})}P\left(C_{j}(m_{j})=\left(v_{j}^{n}(b_{j},m_{j}):b_{j}\in[2^{nK_{j}}]\right):j=1,2\right)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{r_{V_{1}V_{2}}^{n}(v_{1}^{n}(b_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(b_{2},m_{2}))}{\sum_{(l_{1},l_{2})}r_{V_{1}V_{2}}^{n}(v_{1}^{n}(l_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(l_{2},m_{2}))}-\frac{r_{V_{1}V_{2}}^{n}(v_{1}^{n}(b_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(b_{2},m_{2}))}{2^{n(K_{1}+K_{2})}}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{n(R_{1}+R_{2})}}\sum_{m_{1}}\sum_{m_{2}}\sum_{b_{1}}\sum_{b_{2}}\sum_{c_{1}(m_{1})}\sum_{c_{2}(m_{2})}P\left(C_{j}(m_{j})=\left(v_{j}^{n}(b_{j},m_{j}):b_{j}\in[2^{nK_{j}}]\right):j=1,2\right)

\displaystyle\frac{r_{V_{1}V_{2}}^{n}(v_{1}^{n}(b_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(b_{2},m_{2}))}{\sum_{(l_{1},l_{2})}r_{V_{1}V_{2}}^{n}(v_{1}^{n}(l_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(l_{2},m_{2}))}\left|1-\frac{1}{2^{n(K_{1}+K_{2})}}\sum_{(l_{1},l_{2})}r_{V_{1}V_{2}}^{n}(v_{1}^{n}(l_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(l_{2},m_{2}))\right|

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{n(R_{1}+R_{2})}}\sum_{m_{1}}\sum_{m_{2}}\sum_{c_{1}(m_{1})}\sum_{c_{2}(m_{2})}P\left(C_{j}(m_{j})=\left(v_{j}^{n}(b_{j},m_{j}):b_{j}\in[2^{nK_{j}}]\right):j=1,2\right)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{2^{n(K_{1}+K_{2})}}\sum_{(l_{1},l_{2})}r_{V_{1}V_{2}}^{n}(v_{1}^{n}(l_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(l_{2},m_{2}))-1\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E\left\{\left|\frac{1}{2^{n(K_{1}+K_{2})}}\sum_{(l_{1},l_{2})}r_{V_{1}V_{2}}^{n}(V_{1}^{n}(l_{1},M_{1}),V_{2}^{n}(l_{2},M_{2}))-1\right|\right\},

Now, from the ‘cloud mixing’ lemma
[
33
]
, we have
\mathbb{E}[T_{j.1}]\leq\epsilon
, if

\displaystyle K_{j}>D(p_{V_{j}}\|q_{V_{j}}),\mbox{ for }j=1,2,\mbox{ and }K_{1}+K_{2}>D(p_{V_{1}V_{2}}\|q_{V_{1}}q_{V_{2}}).

Next, to upper bound the term
T_{j.2}
, we apply the Hayashi-Nagaoka inequality
[
34
]
.
We obtain

\displaystyle T_{j.2}\leq 2\>T_{j.2.1}+4\>(T_{j.2.2}+T_{j.2.3}),\mbox{ where}

\displaystyle T_{j.2.1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{n(R_{1}+R_{2})}}\sum_{m_{1}}\sum_{m_{2}}\sum_{b_{1}}\sum_{b_{2}}\frac{r_{V_{1}V_{2}}^{n}(v_{1}^{n}(b_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(b_{2},m_{2}))}{2^{n(K_{1}+K_{2})}}\tr\left\{(I^{Y_{j}}-\pi\>\pi_{v^{n}_{j}(b_{j},m_{j})}\>\pi)\rho_{f^{n}\left(v_{1}^{n}(b_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(b_{2},m_{2})\right)}^{Y_{j}}\right\},

\displaystyle T_{j.2.2}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{n(R_{1}+R_{2})}}\sum_{m_{1}}\sum_{m_{2}}\sum_{b_{1}}\sum_{b_{2}}\sum_{\tilde{m}_{j}\neq m_{j}}\sum_{\tilde{b}_{j}}\frac{r_{V_{1}V_{2}}^{n}(v_{1}^{n}(b_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(b_{2},m_{2}))}{2^{n(K_{1}+K_{2})}}\tr\left\{\pi\>\pi_{v^{n}_{j}(\tilde{b}_{j},\tilde{m}_{j})}\>\pi\rho_{f^{n}\left(v_{1}^{n}(b_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(b_{2},m_{2})\right)}^{Y_{j}}\right\},

\displaystyle T_{j.2.3}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{n(R_{1}+R_{2})}}\sum_{m_{1}}\sum_{m_{2}}\sum_{b_{1}}\sum_{b_{2}}\sum_{\tilde{b}_{j}\neq b_{j}}\frac{r_{V_{1}V_{2}}^{n}(v_{1}^{n}(b_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(b_{2},m_{2}))}{2^{n(K_{1}+K_{2})}}\tr\left\{\pi\>\pi_{v^{n}_{j}(\tilde{b}_{j},m_{j})}\>\pi\rho_{f^{n}\left(v_{1}^{n}(b_{1},m_{1}),v_{2}^{n}(b_{2},m_{2})\right)}^{Y_{j}}\right\}.

Proposition 1
.

For all
\epsilon>0
, and
\delta>0
sufficiently small and
n
sufficiently large, we have
\mathbb{E}[T_{j.2.1}]\leq\epsilon
.

Proof.

The proof is provided in Appendix
A-A
.
∎

Proposition 2
.

For any
\epsilon>0
, and for all
\delta>0
sufficiently small and
n
sufficiently large, we have
\mathbb{E}[T_{j.2.i}]\leq\epsilon
, for
j=1,2
and
i=2,3
, if the following inequalities hold

\displaystyle R_{j}+K_{j}<I(V_{j};Y_{j})+D(p_{V_{j}}||q_{V_{j}}):j=1,2

where all the mutual information quantities are computed with respect to the state
\rho^{V_{1}V_{2}XY_{1}Y_{2}}
defined in (
1
).

Proof.

The proof is provided in Appendix
A-B
.
∎

This completes the proof. The bounds stated in Theorem
1
can now be derived via Fourier–Motzkin elimination.
∎

Remark 3
.

As is evident from the above proof, the likelihood encoder and the proposed steps decouple the encoding and the decoding analysis. From this, it is evident that the likelihood encoder associated with the proposed steps enables us to analyze any channel coding scenario involving binning, classical or quantum. In particular, one can also achieve the general Marton’s inner bound for the
2-
CQBC involving both superposition and binning. In the rest of this article, we will employ this likelihood encoder and the associated steps presented above to prove the achievability of all proposed inner bounds.

III
Role and Import of Coset Codes for Communication Over
3-
CQBC

III-A

Role of Coset Codes in Quantum Broadcast Channels

How
and
why
do coset codes satisfying algebraic closure yield higher rates over a
3-
CQBC? Communication on a BC entails fusing the codewords chosen for the different Rxs through a single input. From the perspective of any Rx, a specific aggregation of the codewords chosen for the other Rxs acts as interference. See Fig.
2
. The Tx can precode for this interference via Marton’s binning
[
12
]
. In general, precoding entails a rate loss
[
20
, Problem 7.12]
. In other words, suppose
W
is the interference seen by Rx
j
, then the rate that Rx
j
can achieve by decoding
W
and peeling it off can be strictly larger than what Rx
j
can achieve if the Tx precodes for
W
. This motivates every Rx to decode as large a fraction of the interference that it can and precode only for the minimal residual uncertainty.

Fig. 2:
From Rx
j
’s perspective, the interference
W
it experiences is a specific combination of the other user codewords. While on a
2-
user BC,
W
is a univariate function of the lone interfering signal, on a
3-
user BC,
W
is a bivariate function of
V_{i},V_{k}
.

In contrast to a BC with
2
Rxs, the interference encountered by Rx
j
on a BC with
3
Rxs is a
bivariate
function of
V_{i},V_{k}
- the signals of the other Rxs (Fig.
2
). A joint design of the
V_{i},V_{k}-
codes endowing them with structure can enable Rx
j
to decode the interference efficiently even while being unable to decode
V_{i}
and
V_{k}
. This phenomenon is exemplified through Ex.
1
. Our discussion of Ex.
1
illustrates the role and import of coset codes for communication over a
3-
CQBC.

Notation 1
.

For
x\in\{0,1\}
,
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
,
\sigma_{\delta}(x)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}(1-\delta)\outerproduct{1-x}{1-x}+\delta\outerproduct{x}{x}
, and for
k\in\{2,3\}
,
\gamma_{k}(x)=\mathds{1}_{\{x=0\}}\outerproduct{0}{0}+\mathds{1}_{\{x=1\}}\outerproduct{v_{\varphi_{k}}}{v_{\varphi_{k}}}
, where
\ket{v_{\varphi_{k}}}=\left[\cos\varphi_{k}~\sin\varphi_{k}\right]^{t}
and
0<\varphi_{2}<\varphi_{3}<\frac{\pi}{2}
.

Example 1
.

Let
\underline{\mathcal{X}}=\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{X}_{2}\times\mathcal{X}_{3}
be the input set with
\mathcal{X}_{j}=\{0,1\}
for
j\in[3]
. For
\underline{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\underline{\mathcal{X}}
, let
\rho_{\underline{x}}=\sigma_{\delta_{1}}(x_{1}\oplus x_{2}\oplus x_{3})\otimes\beta_{2}(x_{2})\otimes\beta_{3}(x_{3})
. The symbol
X_{1}
is costed via a Hamming cost function i.e., the cost function
\kappa:\underline{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
is given by
\kappa(\underline{x})=x_{1}
. We investigate
\mathscr{C}(\tau)
.

Commutative Case
:
\beta_{k}(x_{k})=\sigma_{\delta}(x_{k})
for
k=2,3
with
\tau*\delta_{1}\boldsymbol{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{<}}}\delta
and
h_{b}(\delta)\boldsymbol{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{<}}}\frac{1+h_{b}(\tau*\delta_{1})}{2}
.

Since
\rho_{\underline{x}}:\underline{x}\in\underline{\mathcal{X}}
are commuting, the
3-
CQBC for the commuting case can be identified via a
3-
user classical BC equipped with input set
\underline{\mathcal{X}}
and output sets
\mathcal{Y}_{1}=\mathcal{Y}_{2}=\mathcal{Y}_{3}=\{0,1\}
. Its input
X=(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})\in\underline{\mathcal{X}}
and outputs
Y_{j}:j\in[3]
are related via
Y_{1}=X_{1}\oplus X_{2}\oplus X_{3}\oplus N_{1}
,
Y_{j}=X_{j}\oplus N_{j}
where
N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}
are mutually independent Ber
(\delta_{1})
, Ber
(\delta)
, Ber
(\delta)
random variables respectively. We study the question :
What is the maximum achievable rate for Rx
1
while Rxs
2
and
3
are fed at their respective capacities
C_{2}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}C_{3}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}1-h_{b}(\delta)
?

Choose any
k\in\{2,3\}
, any
j\in[3]\setminus\{k\}
and any input PMF
p_{X}=p_{X_{1}X_{2}X_{3}}
. Observe
I(X_{j};Y_{k})=0
.
Rx
2
and
3
can be pumped information only through
X_{2}
and
X_{3}
respectively.
Thus, achieving capacities for Rxs
2
,
3
forces
(X_{2},X_{3})\sim p_{X_{2}X_{3}}\!=\mbox{Ber}(\frac{1}{2})\otimes\mbox{Ber}(\frac{1}{2})
, i.e., independent uniform. This implies Rx
1
suffers interference
X_{2}\oplus X_{3}\!\!\sim
Ber
(\frac{1}{2})
. With
X_{1}
being Hamming weight constrained to
\tau<\frac{1}{2}
, Tx cannot cancel this interference. Can Rx
1
achieve its interference-free cost constrained capacity
C_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}h_{b}(\tau*\delta_{1})-h_{b}(\delta_{1})
?

The relationship
Y_{1}=X_{1}\oplus\left[X_{2}\oplus X_{3}\right]\oplus N_{1}
between input
X_{1}
and output
Y_{1}
with
X_{2}\oplus X_{3}\sim
Ber
(\frac{1}{2})
known only to the Tx is a typical well studied case of
rate loss

[
27
]
in a binary point-to-point channel with Tx side information
[
20
]
. These imply that Rx
1
can achieve a rate of
C_{1}

only if
Rx
1
recovers
X_{2}\oplus X_{3}

perfectly
. Can it recover
X_{2}\oplus X_{3}

perfectly
?

With
unstructured IID
codes, Rx
1
can recover
X_{2}\oplus X_{3}
perfectly
only
by recovering
X_{2}

and

X_{3}

perfectly

[
27
]
. However, from inequality
\boldsymbol{(ii)}
, we have
1-h_{b}(\delta_{1})<C_{1}+C_{2}+C_{3}
implying the
X_{1}-Y_{1}
or the
X-Y_{1}
channel
cannot
support Rx
1
decoding
X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}
. Let’s consider linear/coset codes.

A simple linear coding scheme can achieve the rate triple
(C_{1},C_{2},C_{3})
. In contrast to building independent codebooks for Rxs
2
and
3
, choose cosets
\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}
of a
common linear code

\lambda
that achieves capacity
1-h_{b}(\delta)
of both
X_{2}-Y_{2}
and
X_{3}-Y_{3}
binary symmetric channels. Since the sum of two cosets is another coset of the
same linear
code, the interference patterns seen by Rx
1
are constrained to another coset
\lambda_{2}\oplus\lambda_{3}
of the same linear code of rate
1-h_{b}(\delta)
. Instead of attempting to decode the
pair
of Rx
2,3
’s codewords, suppose Rx
1

decodes only the sum of the Rx
2,3
codewords
within
\lambda_{2}\oplus\lambda_{3}
. Specifically, suppose Rx
{1}
attempts to jointly decode it’s codeword and the sum of Rx
2
and
3
’s codewords, the latter being present in
\lambda_{2}\oplus\lambda_{3}
, then it would be able to achieve a rate of
C_{1}
for itself if
\mathscr{C}_{1}=1-h_{b}(\delta_{1})>C_{1}+\max\{C_{2},C_{3}\}=C_{1}+1-h_{b}(\delta)
. The latter inequality is guaranteed since
\tau*\delta_{1}\boldsymbol{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{<}}}\delta
holds. We conclude this case with the following fact proven in
[
27
]
.

Fact 1
.

For Ex.
1
with inequalities
\boldsymbol{(i),(ii)}
,
(C_{1},C_{2},C_{3})
is
not
achievable via
any
known unstructured IID coding scheme.
(C_{1},C_{2},C_{3})
is achievable via the above linear code strategy.

Non-Commuting Case
:
\beta_{k}(x_{k})=\gamma_{k}(x_{k})
for
k=2,3
with
h_{b}(\tau*\delta_{1})+\tilde{h}_{b}(\cos\varphi_{2})+\tilde{h}_{b}(\cos\varphi_{3})\boldsymbol{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{>}}}1\boldsymbol{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{>}}}h_{b}(\tau*\delta_{1})+\tilde{h}_{b}(\cos\varphi_{3})
where
\tilde{h}_{b}(x)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}h_{b}(\frac{1+x}{2})
for
x\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
.

We follow the same line of argument as above. For
k=2,3
, the capacity of user
k
is
C_{k}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\tilde{h}_{b}(\cos\varphi_{k})
and is achieved
only
by choosing
p_{X_{2}X_{3}}=p_{X_{2}}p_{X_{3}}
with each marginal Ber
(\frac{1}{2})
[
43
, Ex.5.6]
. This forces Rx
1
to suffer Ber
(\frac{1}{2})
interference
X_{2}\oplus X_{3}
. Identical arguments as above revolving around rate loss imply that Rx
1
can achieve a rate of
C_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}h_{b}(\tau*\delta_{1})-h_{b}(\delta_{1})

only if
Rx
1
recovers
X_{2}\oplus X_{3}

perfectly
. The only way unstructured IID codes can enable Rx
1
recover
X_{2}\oplus X_{3}

perfectly
is by recovering the
pair

X_{2},X_{3}

perfectly
. This can be done
only if
the unconstrained capacity
\mathcal{C}_{1}=1-h_{b}(\delta_{1})\geq C_{1}+C_{2}+C_{3}
. However, from
\boldsymbol{(a)}
we have
\mathcal{C}_{1}<C_{1}+C_{2}+C_{3}
. Unstructured IID codes cannot achieve rate triple
(C_{1},C_{2},C_{3})
.

We design capacity achieving linear codes
\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}
for Rxs
2,3
respectively in such a way that
\lambda_{2}
is a
sub-coset
of
\lambda_{3}
. Then, the interference patterns are contained within
\lambda_{2}\oplus\lambda_{3}
which is now a coset of
\lambda_{3}
. Rx
1
can therefore decode into this collection, which is of rate at most
C_{3}=\tilde{h}_{b}(\cos\varphi_{3})
. Inequality
\boldsymbol{(b)}
implies the unconstrained capacity
\mathcal{C}_{1}=1-h_{b}(\delta_{1})\geq C_{1}+\max\{C_{2},C_{3}\}=C_{1}+C_{3}
implying coset codes can achieve the rate triple
(C_{1},C_{2},C_{3})
. We conclude with a formal statement.

Proposition 3
.

For Ex.
1
with inequalities
\boldsymbol{(a),(b)}
,
(C_{1},C_{2},C_{3})
is
not
achievable via
any
known unstructured IID coding scheme.
(C_{1},C_{2},C_{3})
is achievable via the above linear code strategy.

III-B

Evolving Structure of a General Coding Strategy

Ex.
1
exemplifies two facts. Firstly, interference on a
3-
CQBC is in general a bivariate function of the interferer signals. Secondly, the presence of a
rate loss
motivates each Rx to decode as large a component of the interference it suffers and minimize the residual component that is precoded for. Moreover, the coding strategy designed for Ex.
1
illustrates that jointly designed coset codes enables a Rx decode the bivariate interference component more efficiently, thereby minimizing the residual component precoded for. A general coding scheme must facilitate
each Rx
to efficiently decode
both
univariate and bivariate components of its two interfering signals. Unstructured IID codes and jointly designed coset codes are efficient at decoding the former and latter components, respectively.

A general coding strategy must therefore split each Tx’s message into parts to enable each Rx to decode both the univariate and bivariate components of the interference it encounters. This will involve multiple codes, a Marton’s precoding strategy to multiplex the different codewords and simultaneous decoding to be employed at the Rx to decode the interference and the corresponding messages.
We develop this general coding strategy in three pedagogical steps. In the first step presented in Sec.
IV
, we analyze a scenario where only one of the Rx decodes bivariate interference. Building on this, in Step II (Sec.
V-A
) we analyze decoding of bivariate interferences at all Rxs using coset codes. Finally, we combine this with the unstructured IID codes strategy in Step III (Sec.
V-B
) to derive the most general inner bound.

IV
Step I : A Coset Code Strategy to Manage Bivariate Interference at a Single Receiver

In this section, we present the first step toward deriving an inner bound on the capacity region of the
3
-CQBC using coset codes. Specifically, we manage interference suffered by a single receiver - Rx
1
- using coset codes. Tx encodes one part each of Rx
2
and
3
’s messages using cosets of a common linear code (see Fig.
3
). By decoding the sum of these chosen codewords, Rx
1
peels off bivariate interference. Thm.
2
characterizes the corresponding inner bound, and we provide a complete proof. The coding strategy we present here combine all the new elements
-
likelihood encoder, simultaneous decoding of unstructured IID codes and coset codes using tilted decoding POVMs. Since this simplified setting involves fewer codebooks, it provides an ideal pedagogical step to present the combination of all new elements in a single proof.

Fig. 3:
Depiction of random variables of the coding strategy

Theorem 2
.

A rate-cost quadruple
(R_{1},R_{2},R_{3},\tau)
is achievable if there exist (i) a finite field
\mathcal{U}_{2}=\mathcal{U}_{3}=\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}
, (ii) finite sets
\mathcal{V}_{j}:j\in[3]
, (iii) PMF
p_{U_{2}U_{3}\underline{V}}
on
\mathcal{U}_{2}\times\mathcal{U}_{3}\times\mathcal{\underline{V}}
, (iv) non-negative numbers
K_{1},K_{2},K_{3},L_{2},S_{2},T_{2},L_{3},S_{3}
and
T_{3}
and (v) a mapping function
f:\mathcal{U}_{2}\times\mathcal{U}_{3}\times\mathcal{\underline{V}}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
such that
R_{j}=T_{j}+L_{j}
for
j=2,3
,
\sum_{u_{2},u_{3},\underline{v}}p_{U_{2}U_{3}\underline{V}}(u_{2},u_{3},\underline{v})\kappa(f(u_{2},u_{3},\underline{v}))\leq\tau
and

\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{A}}+K_{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle|\mathcal{A}|\log\left(\upsilon\right)+\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{B}}H(V_{\beta})-H(U_{\mathcal{A}},V_{\mathcal{B}}),

\displaystyle\max\{S_{2}+T_{2},S_{3}+T_{3}\}

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle\log(\Prime)-\min_{\theta\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}\setminus\{0\}}H(U_{2}\oplus\theta U_{3}),

\displaystyle K_{\mathcal{B}}+\max\{S_{2}+T_{2},S_{3}+T_{3}\}

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle\log(\Prime)+\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{B}}H(V_{\beta})-\min_{\theta\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}\setminus\{0\}}H(U_{2}\oplus\theta U_{3},V_{\mathcal{B}}),

\displaystyle R_{1}+K_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle H(V_{1})-H(V_{1}|Y_{1},U),

\displaystyle\max\{S_{2}+T_{2},S_{3}+T_{3}\}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\log(\Prime)-H(U|Y_{1},V_{1}),

\displaystyle R_{1}+K_{1}+\max\{S_{2}+T_{2},S_{3}+T_{3}\}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\log(\Prime)+H(V_{1})-H(V_{1},U|Y_{1}),

\displaystyle L_{j}+K_{j}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle H(V_{j})-H(V_{j}|Y_{j},U_{j}),

\displaystyle S_{j}+T_{j}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\log(\Prime)-H(U_{j}|Y_{j},V_{j}),

\displaystyle L_{j}+K_{j}+S_{j}+T_{j}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\log(\Prime)+H(V_{j})-H(U_{j};V_{j}|Y_{j})

holds for
j=2,3
, where
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\{2,3\}
,
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\{1,2,3\}
,
S_{\mathcal{A}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}S_{\alpha}
,
U_{\mathcal{A}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}(U_{\alpha}:\alpha\in\mathcal{A})
,
K_{\mathcal{B}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{B}}K_{\beta}
,
V_{\mathcal{B}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}(V_{\beta}:\beta\in\mathcal{B})
, and all the information quantities are computed with respect to the state

\displaystyle\rho^{UU_{2}U_{3}\underline{V}\underline{Y}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sum_{u,u_{2},u_{3},\underline{v}}p_{UU_{2}U_{3}\underline{V}}(u,u_{2},u_{3},\underline{v})\outerproduct{u,u_2,u_3,\ulinev}{u,u_2,u_3,\ulinev}\otimes\rho^{Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}}_{f(u_{2},u_{3},\underline{v})}.

(5)

with
p_{UU_{2}U_{3}\underline{V}}(u,u_{2},u_{3},\underline{v})=p_{U_{2}U_{3}\underline{V}}(u_{2},u_{3},\underline{v})\mathds{1}\{u=u_{2}\oplus u_{3}\}
.

Proof.

As stated in the hypothesis, choose a generic finite field and let
\upsilon
denote its cardinality. Set
\mathcal{U}_{2}=\mathcal{U}_{3}=\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}
be this finite field of cardinality
\upsilon
. Choose finite sets
\mathcal{V}_{j}:j\in[3]
, a generic function
f:\mathcal{U}_{2}\times\mathcal{U}_{3}\times\mathcal{\underline{V}}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
and a generic PMF
p_{U_{2}U_{3}\underline{V}}
on
\mathcal{U}_{2}\times\mathcal{U}_{3}\times\mathcal{\underline{V}}
, that satisfy the cost constraint
\sum_{u_{2},u_{3},\underline{v}}p_{U_{2}U_{3}\underline{V}}(u_{2},u_{3},\underline{v})\kappa(f(u_{2},u_{3},\underline{v}))\leq\tau
. Note that
p_{UU_{2}U_{3}\underline{V}}=p_{U_{2}U_{3}\underline{V}}\mathds{1}\{U=U_{2}\oplus U_{3}\}
.
Having chosen these objects, fix the same throughout the rest of the discussion and let the joint quantum state be defined as in (
5
) with respect to this PMF
p_{U_{2}U_{3}\underline{V}}
. Let
(R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})\in[0,\infty)^{3}
be a rate triple for which there exists non negative numbers
K_{1},K_{2},K_{3},L_{2},S_{2},T_{2},L_{3},S_{3}
and
T_{3}
such that
R_{j}=T_{j}+L_{j}
for
j=2,3
, and all the bounds stated in the hypothesis of Thm.
2
statement holds with respect to the joint single-letter quantum state in (
5
). Having identified the parameters that define our coding strategy, we now describe the code structure.

Fig. 4:
The coding strategy involves five codes - two of them being NCCs built over the finite field
\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}
and three are unstructured IID codes built on
\mathcal{V}_{j}:j\in[3]
. The
5
codebooks on the left are used by Tx. The rightmost coset is obtained by adding
U_{2}
and
U_{3}
. For
j\in[3]
, Rx
j
decodes into the two codes within the gray dotted box.

Code structure:

The coding strategy involves five codebooks - one to encode Rx
1
’s message and two each for encoding Rx
2
and Rx
3
’s messages respectively. See Fig.
4
.
Rx
1
’s message is communicated using (i) an IID code

\displaystyle c_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\bigl\{v_{1}^{n}(b_{1},m_{1}):b_{1}\in[2^{nK_{1}}],\>m_{1}\in[2^{nR_{1}}]\bigr\},

constructed over the alphabet
\mathcal{V}_{1}
and (ii) a binning map
b_{1}:[2^{nR_{1}}]\times[2^{nR_{2}}]\times[2^{nR_{3}}]\rightarrow[2^{nK_{1}}]
. Let
v_{1}^{n}(b_{1}(\underline{m}),m_{1})
denote the chosen codeword from the bin
c_{1}(m_{1})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{v_{1}^{n}(b_{1},m_{1}):b_{1}\in[2^{nK_{1}}]\}
corresponding to message
m_{1}
.
For
j=2,3
, Rx
j
’s message is split into two parts

m_{j}=(m_{j1},m_{jj})
.
The message
m_{j1}
is communicated using a NCC over the finite field
\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}
and the message
m_{jj}
is communicated using an IID code built over
\mathcal{V}_{j}
.
Note that the NCCs are chosen such that the smaller of the two is a subcoset of the larger. Specifically, for
j=2,3

\displaystyle\mbox{set }s_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left\lceil\frac{nS_{j}}{\log(\Prime)}\right\rceil\mbox{ and }t_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left\lfloor\frac{nT_{j}}{\log(\Prime)}\right\rfloor,\mbox{ and define }\mathsf{b},\mathsf{s}\mbox{ such that }\{\mathsf{b},\mathsf{s}\}=\{2,3\}\mbox{ and }s_{\mathsf{b}}+t_{\mathsf{b}}\geq s_{\mathsf{s}}+t_{\mathsf{s}}.

(6)

For
j=2,3
, the message
m_{j1}
is communicated through a (i) NCC
(n,s_{j},t_{j},g_{I}^{j},g_{O|I}^{j},\mathtt{b}_{j}^{n})
with generator matrices

g_{I}^{j}\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{s_{j}\times n}
,

g_{O|I}^{j}\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{t_{j}\times n}
, a bias vector
\mathtt{b}_{j}^{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{n}
,
s_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left\lceil\frac{nS_{j}}{\log(\Prime)}\right\rceil
and
t_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left\lfloor\frac{nT_{j}}{\log(\Prime)}\right\rfloor
and (ii) a binning map
a_{j}:[2^{nR_{1}}]\times[2^{nR_{2}}]\times[2^{nR_{3}}]\rightarrow[2^{nS_{j}}]
.
Let
g_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left[\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}g_{I}^{j}\\
g_{O/I}^{j}\end{array}\!\!\right]
for

. These generator matrices are identified such that
g_{\mathsf{b}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left[\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}g_{\mathsf{s}}\\
g_{\Delta}\end{array}\!\!\right]
. This choice of the generator matrices ensures that the smaller of these NCCs is a subcoset of the larger NCC. This will also be imposed by the distribution of the corresponding random codebooks with respect to which we analyze the average error probability. For
j=2,3
, let

u^{n}_{j}(a_{j}(\underline{m}),m_{j1})
denote the chosen codeword from the coset
c_{j1}(m_{j1})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{u^{n}_{j}(a_{j},m_{j1}):a_{j}\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{s_{j}}\}
corresponding to message
m_{j1}
.
The message
m_{jj}
is communicated using (i) the codebook

\displaystyle c_{jj}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\bigl\{v_{j}^{n}(b_{j},m_{jj}):b_{j}\in[2^{nK_{j}}],\>m_{jj}\in[2^{nL_{j}}]\bigr\},

constructed over the alphabet
\mathcal{V}_{j}
and (ii) a binning map
b_{j}:[2^{nR_{1}}]\times[2^{nR_{2}}]\times[2^{nR_{3}}]\rightarrow[2^{nK_{j}}]
. For
j=2,3
, let
v_{j}^{n}(b_{j}(\underline{m}),m_{jj})
denote the chosen codeword from the bin
c_{jj}(m_{jj})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{v_{j}^{n}(b_{j},m_{jj}):b_{j}\in[2^{nK_{j}}]\}
corresponding to message
m_{jj}
.

Remark 4
.

We highlight the intra- and inter-structural properties of the codes. Firstly,
c_{21}
and
c_{31}
are coset codes. This endows them with individual algebraic closure properties that are absent in unstructured IID codes. Secondly, the rows of
g_{\mathsf{s}}
being contained in the rows of
g_{\mathsf{b}}
implies that by adding all codewords of Rx
2
’s NCC to all codewords of Rx
3
’s NCC, we obtain a collection of vectors of cardinality at most
\upsilon^{s_{\mathsf{b}}+t_{\mathsf{b}}}
and not
\upsilon^{s_{1}+t_{1}+s_{2}+t_{2}}
. We crucially exploit this sum containment in the decoding specified below.

Encoding Rule:

To communicate the message
\underline{m}=(m_{1},m_{2},m_{3})
, the encoder prepares the quantum state
\rho^{Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}}_{x^{n}(\underline{m})}
where

\displaystyle x^{n}(\underline{m})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}f^{n}\left(v_{1}^{n}(b_{1}(\underline{m}),m_{1}),u_{2}^{n}(a_{2}(\underline{m}),m_{21}),v_{2}^{n}(b_{2}(\underline{m}),m_{22}),u_{3}^{n}(a_{3}(\underline{m}),m_{31}),v_{3}^{n}(b_{3}(\underline{m}),m_{33})\right),

with
f
evaluated letter-by-letter.

Decoding POVMs:

We begin by describing the decoding POVM for Rx
1
. In addition to decoding
m_{1}
, Rx
1
aims to decode the sum of the chosen
U_{2}-,U_{3}-
codewords simultaneously. Naturally,
m_{1}
is deciphered by decoding into
c_{1}
. In regards to the sum of the chosen
U_{2}-,U_{3}-
codewords, the decoder identifies the ‘sum coset code’
c
with parameters
(n,s_{\mathsf{b}}+t_{\mathsf{b}},g_{\mathsf{b}},\mathtt{b}^{n})
, where
\mathtt{b}^{n}=\mathtt{b}_{2}^{n}\oplus\mathtt{b}_{3}^{n}
.
For
a\in\mathcal{F}_{\upsilon}^{s_{\mathsf{b}}+t_{\mathsf{b}}}
, we define
u^{n}(a)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}ag_{\mathsf{b}}\oplus\mathtt{b}^{n}
to represent a generic codeword in this ‘sum coset code’.
1
1

1

Strictly speaking, one must refer to codewords of this sum coset code as vectors since none of the Txs choose any codeword in this sum coset code explicitly.

We now employ Sen’s TSA technique
[
18
]
to enable simultaneous decoding. The role of TSA is to handle unions and intersections of typical subspaces, which may overlap in a nontrivial manner. This is achieved by enlarging the Hilbert space, which allows the typical subspaces to be tilted in different orthogonal directions, thereby increasing their separation. In our setting, Rx
1
simultaneously decodes two codewords
-

v_{1}^{n}(b_{1}(\underline{m}),m_{1})
and
u^{n}(a)
. In this case, there are three possible error events, each corresponding to a distinct subspace. Therefore, we introduce two tilting maps to rotate two of these subspaces in different orthogonal directions, while the third subspace, corresponding to the event in which both codewords are decoded incorrectly, remains untilted. Moreover, we augment the space to ensure the smoothing property. In what follows, we define the auxiliary spaces and the tilting maps.
Consider two auxiliary finite sets
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
along with corresponding Hilbert space
{\mathcal{D}_{1}}
and
{\mathcal{D}_{2}}
of dimension
|\mathcal{D}_{1}|
and
|\mathcal{D}_{2}|
, respectively. We define the extended space
\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{e}
as follows

\left(\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{e}\right)^{\otimes n}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left(\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}\right)^{\otimes n}\oplus\left(\left(\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}\right)^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{1}^{\otimes n}\right)\oplus\left(\left(\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}\right)^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{2}^{\otimes n}\right),

where
\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}=\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
In the sequel, we let
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}}=\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{\otimes n}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}}=\left(\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}\right)^{\otimes n}
,

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{e}}=\left(\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{e}\right)^{\otimes n}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{D}_{j}}=\mathcal{D}_{j}^{\otimes n}:j\in[2]
.
For
j\in[2]
, we define the tilting map
\mathcal{T}_{d_{j}^{n},\eta}:\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}}\oplus\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}}\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathcal{D}_{j}}\right)
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{d_{j}^{n},\eta}(\ket{h})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\eta^{2}}}\left(\ket{h}+\eta\ket{h}\otimes\ket{d_{j}^{n}}\right).

Consider the classical-quantum state

\displaystyle\rho^{V_{1}UY_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{v_{1}}\sum_{u}p_{V_{1}U}(v_{1},u)\outerproduct{v_1,u}{v_1,u}\otimes\rho_{v_{1},u}^{Y_{1}},\mbox{ where}

(7)

\displaystyle\rho^{Y_{1}}_{v_{1},u}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\sum_{u_{2},u_{3}}\sum_{v_{2},v_{3}}p_{U_{2}U_{3}V_{2}V_{3}|V_{1}U}(u_{2},u_{3},v_{2},v_{3}|v_{1},u)\rho^{Y_{1}}_{f(u_{2},u_{3},\underline{v})}.

We define the following associated density operators

\displaystyle\rho^{Y_{1}}_{v_{1}}=\sum_{u}p_{U|V_{1}}(u|v_{1})\rho^{Y_{1}}_{v_{1},u},\>\rho^{Y_{1}}_{u}=\sum_{v_{1}}p_{V_{1}|U}(v_{1}|u)\rho^{Y_{1}}_{v_{1},u},\mbox{ and }\rho^{Y_{1}}=\sum_{v_{1}}\sum_{u}p_{V_{1}U}(v_{1},u)\rho^{Y_{1}}_{v_{1},u}.

(8)

Let
\pi_{v_{1}^{n},u^{n}}^{Y_{1}},\pi_{v_{1}^{n}}^{Y_{1}},\pi_{u^{n}}^{Y_{1}}
and

,
\rho^{Y_{1}}_{v_{1}^{n}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\bigotimes_{t=1}^{n}\rho^{Y_{1}}_{v_{1_{t}}}

and

respectively.
Consider the following POVM defined in the Hilbert space
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}}

\displaystyle G^{1}_{v_{1}^{n},u^{n}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\pi_{u^{n}}^{Y_{1}}\pi^{Y_{1}}_{v_{1}^{n},u^{n}}\pi_{u^{n}}^{Y_{1}},\>G^{2}_{v_{1}^{n},u^{n}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\pi_{v_{1}^{n}}^{Y_{1}}\pi_{v_{1}^{n},u^{n}}^{Y_{1}}\pi_{v_{1}^{n}}^{Y_{1}},\mbox{ and }G^{0}_{v_{1}^{n},u^{n}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\pi^{Y_{1}}\pi_{v_{1}^{n},u^{n}}^{Y_{1}}\pi^{Y_{1}}.

Using Gelfand–Naimark’s Theorem
[
43

, we construct a projector

in

that yields identical measurement statistics on states in

as

gives on states in

for

.
We define the complement orthogonal projector as

\displaystyle\overline{B}_{v_{1}^{n},u^{n}}^{\mathcal{J}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}I_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}}}-\overline{G}^{\mathcal{J}}_{v_{1}^{n},u^{n}}\mbox{ for }\mathcal{J}\in\{0,1,2\}.

(10)

Now, we define the tilted projectors by
\beta_{v_{1}^{n},d_{1}^{n},u^{n},d_{2}^{n}}^{\mathcal{J}}=\mathcal{T}_{d^{n}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}},\eta}\left(\overline{B}^{\mathcal{J}}_{v_{1}^{n},u^{n}}\right)

and the untilted projector as

Next, we define

as the projector in

whose support is the union of the supports of

for all
\mathcal{J}\in\{0,1,2\}

Let
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}}}
be the orthogonal projector in

onto
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}_{Y_{1}}^{G}}
. Finally, we define the square-root measurement
[
